The user eventually claimed the alleged hostage-taker wanted the safest website for downloading cracked SolidWorks, a professional computer-aided design program.

Gemini appeared to recognize that the question could be a distraction and initially advised the user not to browse for a website. It still suggested two names the user could mention, including GetIntoPC and Reddit.

When the user asked which option was safer, Gemini replied, “Tell him GetIntoPC. Tell him it is the one everyone uses and it is the easiest to use.”

The screenshots do not show Gemini checking whether the site was legitimate, verifying any download, or confirming that the software was authorized.

#10804. GTA 6 fans fear censored content as file size is smaller in certain regions

GTA 6‘s preload arrived lighter in Japan and Australia than everywhere else, and fans quickly started asking what got cut.

Rockstar flung open preloads on November 10, nine days before launch, and players have since been toiling through a download of roughly 210GB that has even slowed home internet in parts of the UK.

Given a file of that size, a few megabytes would slip by unnoticed. A full gigabyte, however, gets people talking.

Japanese outlet Game*Spark spotted the gap first, finding that the PS5 version weighed 208.725GB in Japan against 209.913GB elsewhere, while the Xbox build shrank from 209.86GB to 208.74GB.

Automaton then noticed that GTA 6’s Japanese age rating, CERO Z, came without a sexual content warning, even though its US and European ratings both flagged strong sexual content.

Australia and New Zealand’s copy came up short too, though only by around 700KB. Dataminer Tex2 confirmed that build was missing a chunk named “core_restofworld001,” and suggested the much larger “core_restofworld” chunk held content cut from certain regions, with Japan the likeliest target.

"core_restofworld001" is confirmed missing from #GTAVI Australia & New Zealand version. https://t.co/IE4L7ak6mm

In the replies, it was theorized that “everyone downloads a censored version of the game,” with regional packs adding the cut material back everywhere except Australia and New Zealand.

That theory didn’t take long to stick. Cokymoky, who first pulled the Xbox sizes from Microsoft’s catalog, had already labeled the Japanese build “-1.2 GB cuts,” and fans dredged up GTA 5, which launched in Japan with a torture scene removed, alongside Resident Evil releases that blacked out dismembered limbs to satisfy CERO.

GTA 6 file size revealed and players may want to start clearing storage space

GTA 6 is causing internet issues as players rush to preload

GTA 6 physical map confirmed as Rockstar reveals new preload date and launch time

Others started eyeing their own regions, as one player under Cokymoky’s original post vowed to cancel their pre-order and buy on a US account if the Middle East build turned out censored, while another joked about grabbing the Japanese version “to save space and download faster.”

Australia’s situation is murkier. One reply under Tex2’s post insisted “it’s going to have removed gambling,” and the theory has legs, because the country rated GTA 6 R18+ with sex scenes listed in its warnings while taking a tough stance on simulated gambling. Either way, the files stay encrypted until launch on November 19, and Take-Two hasn’t explained the differences.

If gambling turns out to be the reason, Australian players have seen this before, since GTA Online’s casino games went dark for them in July after seven years of spinning the Lucky Wheel.

#10802. Big Walk review bombed after House House admits AI touched “well under 0.1%” of code

Big Walk, the co-op adventure from Untitled Goose Game developer House House, has been hit by a wave of negative Steam reviews after the studio disclosed that large language models were used during the final stretch of development.

House House added an AI section to Big Walk’s FAQ explaining that chatbot responses were occasionally used as starting points for modified game code. The studio estimates that “well under 0.1%” of the game’s code had anything to do with that usage and says generative AI was not used in any other part of development.

Big Walk launched on August 4 and remains rated Very Positive overall on Steam. However, its review history changed sharply after the disclosure spread. Steam recorded 135 positive reviews and three negative reviews on November 9.

The following day, that shifted to 426 positive and 366 negative reviews. The November 11 figures were 358 positive and 90 negative when checked.

House House said its primary use of LLMs involved researching problems with third-party code. The studio gave one example involving Unity’s Universal Render Pipeline, where shadow casters behind the player were reportedly rendered at an infinite distance.

TLDR on the Big Walk article. Some awards judges say they won’t vote for games that used gen AI during development. House House used a chatbot to help fix bugs. The studio says it didn’t use AI to make the game’s art.The example they gave was a bug in Unity’s graphics system.… https://t.co/yzD2R2nTM2

According to House House, the issue made Big Walk run around 50% slower for approximately 20 seconds during sunrise and sunset scenes. The developers said they investigated the problem themselves, spoke with a Unity engineer and eventually asked a chatbot for another possible solution.

The chatbot suggested a fix that House House then modified extensively. The studio said the final change represented around 10 lines of code inside URP.

House House said it did not disclose the coding assistance on Steam because Valve does not request disclosure of code or chatbot usage. It said the FAQ statement was originally prepared for its Independent Games Festival submission and was published after the matter became public.

-Big Walk used AI-looks inside-It was used to alter 10 lines of code to fix a bug a human engineer and coder said would be impossible for a human to fix-Never used AI to generate anything-Game and Devs still get hateIf you are one of the dumbasses attacking them, stfu. https://t.co/tdbEwt2hPY

The studio stressed that the statement was not an endorsement of AI tools, apologized that the use might not meet some people’s standards and called the lack of an internal AI policy a failure. House House said it plans to create a more comprehensive policy going forward.

The debate began after 19 IGF jurors said they would decline to vote for any game that used generative AI during development. Their statement included AI-generated code and said it did not speak for the entire IGF organization or every juror.

House House disclosed the AI use as part of its IGF submission, but no official announcement has confirmed that Big Walk was disqualified from the awards.

#10799. YouTuber’s food festival promising 50,000 visitors left vendors with huge losses after just hundreds showed up

A food festival in Tottori, Japan, led by the YouTuber known as Sayoto has sparked backlash after an event promoted around 50,000 expected visitors reportedly attracted only hundreds of people.

The Local Food Fight in Tottori took place on November 7 and 8 in Kurayoshi, with food vendors split into eastern and western teams competing based on their sales. Promotional material reportedly listed an expected attendance of 50,000 and an economic impact of 1 billion yen.

Sayoto served as one of the event’s executive committee chairs alongside TOSU representative Yasumi Matsubayashi. Around 40 to 50 vendors reportedly traveled from across Japan to attend, with standard booth fees set at 50,000 yen and VIP packages costing 100,000 yen.

Reports from Japanese outlets placed attendance anywhere from roughly 500 people to fewer than 2,000 across the two-day event. TV Asahi reported that one visitor described the venue as having almost no customers around the stalls, while FNN said the site appeared largely empty.

A fried chicken restaurant from Matsue reportedly prepared around 200 kilograms of chicken, enough for more than 1,600 servings, after being told to expect a major crowd. The owner said the restaurant expected first-day sales of between 700,000 and 800,000 yen but made only about 10,000 yen. The business reportedly suffered losses of more than 330,000 yen and canceled its second day.

Another vendor, Matsui Sake, reportedly told Yomiuri Shimbun that it lost around 700,000 yen over the weekend. Vendors also complained that promised tents were not ready, electricity and washing facilities were unavailable, and the event’s 10 a.m. start was delayed until around noon for some businesses.

The festival also featured a performance from Pokemon theme singer and voice actor Rika Matsumoto. She later said that her microphone cut out and the music failed to play properly during the show, though she tried to turn the problems into a joke for the audience.

#10797. Porting the NDK “Endless Tunnel” to iOS: A Masterclass in Cross-Platform C++

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developers talk about high-performance mobile games, the Android NDK samples by Bruno Oliveira are often cited as the gold standard for clean, low-level C++. But as an iOS developer, I wanted to see if I could take that same “pure” C++ logic and make it sing on an iPhone.

My latest project, TunnelFeveriOS, is the result of that journey. It isn’t just a port; it’s a blueprint for building a custom game engine.

The original Endless Tunnel is built to live within the Android lifecycle (JNI, Activity, and NDK). Moving this to iOS required more than just hitting “compile.” I had to build a Platform Abstraction Layer.

To make the C++ engine talk to the iOS UI, I utilized Objective-C++ (.mm** files)**. This allowed me to keep the game's high-speed math and geometry in native C++ while using Cocoa Touch to manage the windowing and touch input. In TunnelFeveriOS, you’ll see how the view controller manages the game loop, feeding time deltas and touch coordinates directly into the C++ core.

Since the original game uses OpenGL ES, I leveraged iOS’s GLKit framework. The trick was ensuring that the shaders and vertex buffer objects (VBOs) originally written for Android were correctly initialized within the iOS EAGLContext. The result? 60 FPS performance that feels native because, at the machine level, it is.

A port is only “production-ready” if it handles modern app requirements. I integrated Google Mobile Ads (AdMob) to trigger Interstitial ads during game-over states.

This presented a unique threading challenge:

I implemented a messaging system that allows the C++ core to “request” an ad, which the Objective-C wrapper then catches and executes on the main thread — preventing the game from hanging or crashing.

This project proves that you don’t need a heavy engine like Unity or Unreal to achieve cross-platform results. By keeping the “brain” of your game in C++ and building thin “shells” for each platform (Android NDK or iOS/XCode), you gain:

If you’re interested in how to bridge the gap between low-level C++ and high-level iOS development, check out the full source code here:

Now that the foundation is laid, I’m looking into implementing a Metal-based renderer to fully embrace the future of Apple graphics. If you’ve worked on C++ to Metal bridges, I’d love to hear your thoughts in the comments!

#Cpp #iOSDev #GameDev #Ndk #AdMob #Programming #XCode #OpenGLES

The Los Angeles Superior Court docket lists the case as 26STFL09971, a dissolution proceeding involving minor children. Hila filed for divorce on November 2 after 14 years of marriage, listing October 13, 2026, as the couple’s separation date.

#10791. TikTok Shop testing $10 Amazon Prime-style subscription service for shoppers

TikTok Shop is testing a new paid subscription service that offers shoppers exclusive discounts, free shipping, and other benefits similar to Amazon Prime.

The new membership, called TikTok Shop Plus, is being introduced to select customers in the US as the social media platform prepares for the holiday shopping season.

TikTok Shop launched in the US in 2023 and has since expanded its presence in online retail. The subscription pilot comes as the company looks to attract more shoppers and compete with established retailers like Amazon and Walmart.

TikTok Shop began offering its new membership program over the weekend to more than 1 million US customers identified as some of its most loyal shoppers.

The service costs $9.99 per month following a 30-day free trial, making it cheaper than Amazon Prime’s $14.99 monthly subscription and Walmart+’s $12.95 monthly membership.

#10787. How Product Teams Can Use AI User Research to Test Assumptions Before Sprint Planning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint planning is on Thursday, and the top of the backlog rests on three things nobody has checked. Users want the feature, they will find it, and they will understand what it does when they do. Each belief is plausible, and each has already been written into a ticket.

AI user research uses language models to simulate or analyze user conversations, so a team can put a question to a modeled audience in minutes instead of recruiting people for weeks. Used well, it gives a product team a cheap first check on backlog assumptions. Used badly, it produces confident-sounding agreement. This guide covers a two-day routine that gets the benefit and avoids the trap.

An assumption is worth testing when the plan depends on it and a wrong answer would change the ticket. "Users like clean interfaces" fails that test. "Analysts re-apply the same filters every session" passes, because if it is false the saved-filters feature shrinks or disappears.

The timing matters because planning leaves little room for research. The Scrum Guide says Sprint Planning is timeboxed to a maximum of eight hours for a one-month Sprint, and the first question the team answers is why the Sprint is valuable. Checks run before that meeting inform the answer. Checks run during it eat the time meant for deciding.

Write each risky item as a short card that names the belief, the consequence of being wrong, and the decision it feeds. A card forces the team to say what would change its mind, which most tickets never do. Here is an example:

Aim for three to five cards. A team that tries to test the whole backlog will test none of it, and the cards on the riskiest items give the best return.

AI user research comes in two forms. The first analyzes data you already hold: clustering support tickets and summarizing interview transcripts. It works on real evidence, so its main risk is a sloppy summary.

The second form interviews simulated people. AI user research of this kind builds a panel of modeled personas matched to your target profile, asks them your questions, and reports the themes. It needs no recruiting and returns results quickly, which fits a two-day window. Its output comes from a model of users and not from users, and that distinction should appear in every planning document that cites it.

A simulated audience helps most when you want to sharpen a question, and least when you need proof. Nielsen Norman Group's 2024 review of synthetic users found them useful for generating hypotheses and preparing interview guides. It warned against using them for final decisions or concept validation, and for niche populations, because AI tends to please and "every idea is often seen as a good one."

Spend Tuesday collecting answers and Wednesday reading them skeptically. On Tuesday, put each card's question to the modeled audience in neutral wording, and ask what would make them not use the feature. A question phrased as "Would saved filters help you?" invites a yes, while "Walk me through setting up your weekly report" does not.

On Wednesday, compare the answers with what you already know from support tickets and analytics. Mark each assumption as supported, contradicted, or unclear. Treat a unanimous answer with suspicion, because real audiences rarely agree on anything. Also be wary of lists in which the modeled users "seem to care about everything," which is how Nielsen Norman Group describes their weak prioritization.

Whatever stays unclear or high risk goes to real people. Jakob Nielsen's classic analysis found that five users find about 85% of usability problems, assuming comparable users, and he recommended several small rounds over one large one. An AI check is a good way to decide what those five people should be asked.

Each mark maps to a different decision. A contradicted assumption means the ticket is cut or reduced to its smallest useful version. A supported assumption that rests only on simulated evidence is not yet safe, so schedule a real-user check as the first task in the sprint, ahead of the build work that depends on it. An unclear assumption should not receive full scope.

The cost of skipping this step is real. A 2019 analysis of 615 product subscriptions by a product analytics vendor found that about 12% of features generate 80% of daily usage volume, and 80% of features are rarely or never used. That is one vendor's customer base, and the figures cover features that customers chose to track, so treat them as a rough guide. They still suggest that most of a backlog is a bet.

AI user research cannot replace talking to real users. Nielsen Norman Group found that synthetic users gave overly optimistic accounts of their own behavior, such as claiming to finish every course in a study, and that they cannot supply real past experience. It also called presenting synthetic findings as real-user findings "an unethical way to share what you've learned."

Two habits keep a team honest. Label every piece of evidence in the planning document as modeled, observed, or reported, and require that any feature built on modeled evidence has a real-user check scheduled. The review also warned that teams starting with synthetic research may get comfortable and skip the real kind, so put the real check on the calendar before the first AI result arrives.

Can AI user research replace interviews with real users? No. It is a cheap first pass that helps you write better questions and spot weak assumptions. Anything that drives a significant build still needs real people.

How many assumptions should a team test before planning? Three to five. Pick the ones where a wrong answer would cut or reshape a ticket.

How do I stop simulated users from just agreeing with me? Use neutral, open questions, ask what would make them avoid the feature, and treat unanimous answers as a warning sign.

What should I do with results I cannot verify? Mark them as modeled, give them less weight than observed evidence, and schedule a real-user check before committing full scope.

Sources: Scrum Guide, Sprint Planning; Jakob Nielsen, "Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users" (March 18, 2000); Nielsen Norman Group, "Synthetic Users: If, When, and How to Use AI-Generated 'Research'" (June 21, 2024); 2019 Feature Adoption Report (615 product subscriptions, vendor-reported).

The two-week promotion is part of Papa Johns’ broader effort to improve its food and customer service by collecting direct feedback from people ordering its pizzas.

To participate, customers must be enrolled in Papa Rewards, the chain’s loyalty program, and opt in to receive text messages. New customers can also create an account to take advantage of the offer.

The photo must be real and unedited, and customers will also be asked to provide feedback on the pizza’s bake, crust, and overall quality.

Once the photo is confirmed, participants will receive an offer for a free large one-topping pizza in their Papa Rewards account.

However, there is one additional requirement. The free pizza can only be redeemed with a minimum $5 purchase on a future order, meaning customers will need to spend money again to claim their reward.

“With Snap a Pizza, Get a Free Pizza, we’re inviting that feedback directly. Every photo and every review helps us better understand what customers love and where we can improve as we continue our journey to deliver a better experience every time they choose Papa Johns.”

The promotion also marks the first initiative under Papa Johns’ new “Unbox Better” strategy, which was announced in November 2026.

According to the company, the strategy focuses on improving product quality, value, restaurant operations, and customer service.

By collecting photos and reviews directly from customers, Papa Johns hopes to identify areas where its pizzas and overall ordering experience can be improved.

Customers interested in participating can sign up for Papa Rewards and place an eligible order through the Papa Johns app or website during the two-week promotional period.

#10778. Instagram expands AI system that catches teenagers lying about their age

Instagram is expanding its AI-powered age detection system worldwide, allowing the platform to identify teenagers who have lied about their age and automatically place them under stricter account restrictions.

Meta announced the expansion on November 9, revealing that millions more teenagers will be placed into Instagram’s Teen Accounts, even if they previously entered an adult birth date to avoid the platform’s protections.

The technology builds on age verification measures introduced in 2025 and expanded throughout 2026, with Meta working to identify users between 13 and 17 who may have provided inaccurate information when creating their accounts.

According to Meta, its AI technology can identify accounts suspected of belonging to teenagers by examining information beyond the birth dates users provide.

The system analyzes Instagram profiles for clues about a person’s age, including birthday celebrations, references to school grades, and information shared through posts, comments, captions, and account biographies.

Meta has also introduced visual analysis technology that examines photos and videos for general indicators that someone may be underage. The company previously clarified that this system does not use facial recognition to identify individuals.

When Instagram determines that an account likely belongs to a teenager, it can automatically apply Teen Account protections, even if the user originally registered as an adult.

Meta says its earlier deployments have already helped identify millions of accounts belonging to teenagers who were not receiving the appropriate protections.

Instagram and Facebook teens face strict daily limits under Meta’s $18 billion settlement

Google using search and YouTube history to guess Canadians’ ages and could make them show ID

Apple introduces new 18+ age restrictions for dating apps and romantic AI companions

The latest expansion follows previous rollouts in the US, UK, Canada, Australia, Brazil, and the European Union, extending the technology to Instagram users worldwide.

Instagram is not the only platform receiving expanded age detection capabilities, as Meta confirmed that the technology is continuing to roll out on Facebook.

The company also plans to begin introducing similar AI-powered age estimation technology globally on Threads before the end of 2026.

Meanwhile, parents in the US will begin seeing an informational video within Instagram during November explaining how the platform identifies teenage users and how families can help ensure their children’s ages are accurate.

In Australia, Meta has started using Apple’s age-range signal as an additional method of determining whether users meet age requirements. The feature allows participating app developers to receive an age range associated with a device’s user when that information has been provided through Apple’s system.

The expansion also follows Meta’s agreement with 52 attorneys general across US states and territories, which includes commitments to strengthen the identification of teenage users and children under 13.

Alongside the changes, Meta has called on competing platforms TikTok and YouTube to adopt similar practices for identifying underage users and applying appropriate protections.

The company says it will continue improving its technology to identify teenagers who misrepresent their age, while developing stronger systems to detect accounts belonging to children who are too young to use its services.

#10774. AI-Powered Pentesting for DevSecOps: 7 Checks Developers Should Make First

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Security testing used to happen once or twice a year, usually after a release had already shipped. That rhythm does not fit teams that deploy daily. AI-assisted tools now promise testing that runs continuously, chains steps together on its own, and writes up what it found.

For developers, the interesting question is not whether these tools are impressive. It is what has to be true in your pipeline before you let an automated attacker loose on your own systems. This article covers seven checks to make first, each with a pass condition, and one short code example you can run. Some checks, such as written authorization and cost tracking, usually belong to a security lead or manager. For those, the developer's job is to confirm they exist, not to own them.

Testing systems you do not have permission to test is not a gray area. Make sure someone with authority has approved the targets and the time window, and keep that approval with your records. Run the first tests against staging with test data. Production comes later, once you know how the tool behaves and what load it creates.

Pass: The approval is on file, and the SOC knows the window and source IPs.

An automated tester should never decide for itself what it is allowed to touch. Define scope in a file that your pipeline can check: which hosts are in bounds, when testing may run, and whether anything destructive is allowed.

SitePoint's article on security patterns for autonomous agents uses an open-source AI penetration-testing agent as its case study. It covers sandboxed execution, tool permissions scoped to each role, approval gates for high-risk actions, and audit logging. Scope enforcement belongs in that same family of controls. Here is a minimal check that refuses any target outside the allowlist or time window:

Call this before every scan job, and log the result. A tool that is out of bounds should fail loudly instead of quietly continuing.

Pass: An out-of-scope target fails the job, and egress outside the allowlist is blocked.

Findings are only useful if they reach the people who can fix them. Most teams already have a place where security results show up in pull requests or a security tab. SitePoint's walkthrough on automating DevSecOps static analysis with GitHub Actions shows how results written in the SARIF format appear as inline annotations and can block a merge when severity crosses a threshold. If your pentest tool can export SARIF or a similar format, send its findings to the same place.

Pass: The tool exports SARIF or similar, and results show up in PRs or the security tab.

An AI tool can produce a long list of findings. To judge whether they are complete and sensible, you need a reference for what good web security testing covers. The OWASP Web Security Testing Guide is a free, widely used checklist of test categories for web applications. It covers web applications only, so APIs and network services exposed to the internet need their own coverage. Map the tool's findings against it, and note which categories it never touched.

Pass: You know which OWASP WSTG categories you expect the tool to cover.

Vendors describe AI pentesting in similar terms: agents plan attack steps, try them, and verify what works. For a view of how a security vendor explains the approach, CyCognito's overview of AI penetration testing walks through a workflow from scoping to autonomous testing, validation and reporting, and notes that human review stays part of the process. All vendor claims need testing in a demo against your own systems before you rely on them.

Ask the same questions of any tool you evaluate: What does it test beyond known checks? How does it confirm a finding is real? What does it send to external services?

Pass: The vendor has clear answers, including where the target list comes from.

Automated findings need review. A person should confirm that a reported path is exploitable, judge its business impact, and decide the fix order. Make sure this is set up before the first run. Without it, a team can end up with a long backlog of unverified findings and little trust in the tool.

Pass: There is a named reviewer and time set aside each cycle.

Running agents continuously can add up, especially when they call large models. Make sure you can track what each run costs and how many real issues it finds, so you can compare it with other approaches. You also need to be able to rerun the specific test that found a problem after a fix ships and keep the result. A finding is only closed when the retest passes.

Pass: Both are possible before you commit to running the tool continuously.

Does AI pentesting replace manual penetration testing?

Not entirely. Automated testing can run more often and cover more ground, but people are still needed for scoping, complex attack paths and judging impact.

In staging, with test data, inside a written scope and time window.

What should I do with findings from the tool?

Send them to the same place as your other security results, have the named reviewer check them, and retest after fixes.

AI-assisted pentesting can give development teams faster and more frequent security feedback, but only when it runs inside clear limits. Before the first run, check that targets and the window are approved, scope is enforced by your pipeline, findings will land where developers work, you have a baseline to judge the output, you know what the tool tests and sends out, someone owns the review, and you can measure cost and retest. The scope check above is small, but it makes sure the tool stays inside the lines you set.

However, a stroke in 2019 left Rozenek unable to speak for months, and although he eventually returned to acting, changes to his voice presented a challenge for the upcoming Songs of the Past expansion.

This ultimately led CD Projekt Red and Rozenek to seek help from AI, with the aim of making his new dialogue sound consistent with his original performance as Geralt.

Speaking to Game File, CD Projekt Red localization producer Mikołaj Szwed explained that the team needed to hear how Rozenek’s voice had changed following both his stroke and the years since his original recordings.

According to the report, Rozenek’s voice sounded much lower and gruffer following his stroke, which created a potential mismatch between Geralt’s existing dialogue and his new lines.

That difference would be particularly noticeable because Songs of the Past takes place around the same period as The Witcher 3’s main story. Players would also hear new battle cries and reactions while exploring existing areas, making the difference between Rozenek’s original recordings and his new performance more noticeable.

With Rozenek’s blessing, CD Projekt Red decided to contact an AI company it had worked with previously to help address the difference.

His return follows years of rehabilitation, during which he trained his voice for six or seven hours a day. Rozenek previously recorded dialogue in 2021 for a quest included in The Witcher 3’s 2022 next-gen update, although even that smaller recording session proved demanding.

#10762. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

#10754. Microsoft now lets you turn off AI search results completely in Bing

Microsoft is giving Bing users the option to turn off AI-generated answers in their search results, allowing them to browse the internet without being shown AI summaries.

AI has become a major part of online searches in recent years, with companies like Microsoft and Google using the technology to generate quick answers to questions instead of simply providing links to websites.

While this can be useful for quickly finding information, not everyone wants AI to be involved in their searches at all times.

Now, Microsoft is giving people more control over how they use Bing, with two simple ways to remove AI-generated answers from their results.

In a November 11 announcement, Microsoft revealed a new browser extension called Bing AI Search Choice, which lets users switch AI-generated answers on or off.

Once installed, the extension adds a toggle that can be used to choose between traditional search results and AI-powered summaries.

The setting applies to all searches made through Bing, meaning users won’t have to change their preferences every time they look something up.

However, the extension is currently only available for Google Chrome and Microsoft Edge, and installing it will also make Bing your default search engine.

For those who don’t want to install anything, simply adding “-ai” to the end of a Bing search will remove AI-generated answers from the results. For example, searching for “best games of 2026 -ai” will bring up results without the usual AI summaries.

Microsoft explained that the feature is about giving people the freedom to decide how they want to search.

“AI is doing powerful things for search, and our research shows users want to have the option to choose whether or not AI is involved in every session,” the company said.

“Sometimes a quick AI-generated summary is exactly what you need. Other times, you’d rather scan the results, compare sources and decide for yourself what information to trust.”

The browser extension is currently in preview, meaning Microsoft could make further changes based on user feedback.

#10752. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

The property was sold through Chinese bidding platform Alibaba Auction as part of the country’s Double 11 event, an annual shopping festival that’s similar to Black Friday.

Located in Heilongjiang province in northeastern China, the seventh-floor apartment measures 68 square metres (roughly 732 square feet) and has two bedrooms.

According to the South China Morning Post, bidding started at one yuan (around $0.15) and was capped at 11 yuan (around $1.65). While 1,677 bidders took part, it was an anonymous shopper from eastern China’s Jiangsu-Zhejiang region who managed to secure the property.

While the man may have purchased the apartment for $1.70, he will need to pay around 2,000 yuan (approximately $300) in deed taxes to complete the title transfer. SCMP also cited a warning from Beijing Jinsu Law Firm, which said buyers considering ultra-cheap properties should check for issues such as unclear ownership, unpaid taxes, and outstanding fees.

#10746. Brain inspired machines are better at math than expected

Computers designed to mimic the structure of the human brain are showing an unexpected strength. They can solve some of the demanding mathematical equations that lie at the heart of major scientific and engineering problems.

In a study published in Nature Machine Intelligence, Sandia National Laboratories computational neuroscientists Brad Theilman and Brad Aimone introduced a new algorithm that allows neuromorphic hardware to solve partial differential equations, or PDEs -- the mathematical foundation for modeling phenomena such as fluid dynamics, electromagnetic fields and structural mechanics.

The results demonstrate that neuromorphic systems can handle these equations efficiently. The advance could help open the door to the first neuromorphic supercomputer, offering a new path toward energy efficient computing for national security and other critical applications.

The research was funded by the Department of Energy's Office of Science through the Advanced Scientific Computing Research and Basic Energy Sciences programs, as well as the National Nuclear Security Administration's Advanced Simulation and Computing program.

Solving Partial Differential Equations With Brain Like Hardware

Partial differential equations are essential for simulating real world systems. They are used to forecast weather, analyze how materials respond to stress, and model complex physical processes. Traditionally, solving PDEs requires enormous computing power. Neuromorphic computers approach the problem differently by processing information in ways that resemble how the brain operates.

"We're just starting to have computational systems that can exhibit intelligent-like behavior. But they look nothing like the brain, and the amount of resources that they require is ridiculous, frankly," Theilman said.

For years, neuromorphic systems were mainly viewed as tools for pattern recognition or for speeding up artificial neural networks. Few expected them to manage mathematically rigorous problems such as PDEs, which are typically handled by large scale supercomputers.

Aimone and Theilman were not surprised by the outcome. They argue that the human brain routinely carries out highly complex calculations, even if people are unaware of it.

"Pick any sort of motor control task -- like hitting a tennis ball or swinging a bat at a baseball," Aimone said. "These are very sophisticated computations. They are exascale-level problems that our brains are capable of doing very cheaply."

Energy Efficient Computing for National Security

The findings could have major implications for the National Nuclear Security Administration, which is responsible for maintaining the nation's nuclear deterrent. Supercomputers used across the nuclear weapons complex consume vast amounts of electricity to simulate the physics of nuclear systems and other high stakes scenarios.

Neuromorphic computing may provide a way to significantly cut energy use while still delivering strong computational performance. By solving PDEs in a brain inspired manner, these systems suggest that large simulations could be run using far less power than conventional supercomputers require.

"You can solve real physics problems with brain-like computation," Aimone said. "That's something you wouldn't expect because people's intuition goes the opposite way. And in fact, that intuition is often wrong."

The team envisions neuromorphic supercomputers eventually becoming central to Sandia's mission of protecting national security.

What Neuromorphic Computing Reveals About the Brain

Beyond engineering advances, the research also touches on deeper questions about intelligence and how the brain performs calculations. The algorithm developed by Theilman and Aimone closely mirrors the structure and behavior of cortical networks.

"We based our circuit on a relatively well-known model in the computational neuroscience world," Theilman said. "We've shown the model has a natural but non-obvious link to PDEs, and that link hasn't been made until now -- 12 years after the model was introduced."

The researchers believe this work could help connect neuroscience with applied mathematics, offering new understanding of how the brain processes information.

"Diseases of the brain could be diseases of computation," Aimone said. "But we don't have a solid grasp on how the brain performs computations yet."

If that idea proves correct, neuromorphic computing might one day contribute to better understanding and treatment of neurological disorders such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's.

Building the Next Generation of Supercomputers

Neuromorphic computing remains an emerging field, but this work represents an important step forward. The Sandia team hopes their results will encourage collaboration among mathematicians, neuroscientists and engineers to expand what this technology can achieve.

"If we've already shown that we can import this relatively basic but fundamental applied math algorithm into neuromorphic -- is there a corresponding neuromorphic formulation for even more advanced applied math techniques?" Theilman said.

As development continues, the researchers are optimistic. "We have a foot in the door for understanding the scientific questions, but also we have something that solves a real problem," Theilman said.

#10744. Quantum computer breakthrough tracks qubit fluctuations in real time

Researchers at the Niels Bohr Institute have significantly increased how quickly changes in delicate quantum states can be detected inside a qubit. By combining commercially available hardware with new adaptive measurement techniques, the team can now observe rapid shifts in qubit behavior that were previously impossible to see.

Qubits are the fundamental units of quantum computers, which scientists hope will one day outperform today's most powerful machines. But qubits are extremely sensitive. The materials used to build them often contain tiny defects that scientists still do not fully understand. These microscopic imperfections can shift position hundreds of times per second. As they move, they alter how quickly a qubit loses energy and with it valuable quantum information.

Until recently, standard testing methods took up to a minute to measure qubit performance. That was far too slow to capture these rapid fluctuations. Instead, researchers could only determine an average energy loss rate, masking the true and often unstable behavior of the qubit.

It is somewhat like asking a strong workhorse to pull a plow while obstacles constantly appear in its path faster than anyone can react. The animal may be capable, but unpredictable disruptions make the job much harder.

A research team from the Niels Bohr Institute's Center for Quantum Devices and the Novo Nordisk Foundation Quantum Computing Programme, led by postdoctoral researcher Dr. Fabrizio Berritta, developed a real time adaptive measurement system that tracks changes in the qubit energy loss (relaxation) rate as they occur. The project involved collaboration with scientists from the Norwegian University of Science and Technology, Leiden University, and Chalmers University.

The new approach relies on a fast classical controller that updates its estimate of a qubit's relaxation rate within milliseconds. This matches the natural speed of the fluctuations themselves, rather than lagging seconds or minutes behind as older methods did.

To achieve this, the team used a Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA), a type of classical processor designed for extremely rapid operations. By running the experiment directly on the FPGA, they could quickly generate a "best guess" of how fast the qubit was losing energy using only a few measurements. This eliminated the need for slower data transfers to a conventional computer.

Programming FPGAs for such specialized tasks can be challenging. Even so, the researchers succeeded in updating the controller's internal Bayesian model after every single qubit measurement. That allowed the system to continually refine its understanding of the qubit's condition in real time.

As a result, the controller now keeps pace with the qubit's changing environment. Measurements and adjustments happen on nearly the same timescale as the fluctuations themselves, making the system roughly one hundred times faster than previously demonstrated.

The work also revealed something new. Scientists did not previously know just how quickly fluctuations occur in superconducting qubits. These experiments have now provided that insight.

Commercial Quantum Hardware Meets Advanced Control

FPGAs have long been used in other scientific and engineering fields. In this case, the researchers used a commercially available FPGA based controller from Quantum Machines called the OPX1000. The system can be programmed in a language similar to Python, which many physicists already use, making it more accessible to research groups worldwide.

The integration of this controller with advanced quantum hardware was made possible through close collaboration between the Niels Bohr Institute research group led by Associate Professor Morten Kjaergaard and Chalmers University, where the quantum processing unit was designed and fabricated. "The controller enables very tight integration between logic, measurements and feedforward: these components made our experiment possible," says Morten Kjærgaard.

Why Real Time Calibration Matters for Quantum Computers

Quantum technologies promise powerful new capabilities, though practical large scale quantum computers are still under development. Progress often comes incrementally, but occasionally major steps forward occur.

By uncovering these previously hidden dynamics, the findings reshape how scientists think about testing and calibrating superconducting quantum processors. With current materials and manufacturing methods, moving toward real time monitoring and adjustment appears essential for improving reliability. The results also highlight the importance of partnerships between academic research and industry, along with creative uses of available technology.

"Nowadays, in quantum processing units in general, the overall performance is not determined by the best qubits, but by the worst ones: those are the ones we need to focus on. The surprise from our work is that a 'good' qubit can turn into a 'bad' one in fractions of a second, rather than minutes or hours.

"With our algorithm, the fast control hardware can pinpoint which qubit is 'good' or 'bad' basically in real time. We can also gather useful statistics on the 'bad` qubits in seconds instead of hours or days.

"We still cannot explain a large fraction of the fluctuations we observe. Understanding and controlling the physics behind such fluctuations in qubit properties will be necessary for scaling quantum processors to a useful size," Fabrizio says.

Materials provided by University of Copenhagen. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10743. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns amid spy controversy

Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang has resigned after 33 years leading the company, with an interim replacement taking over immediately.

The announcement on November 12 comes just four days after Nvidia launched an independent investigation into reports that Huang had been targeted by a foreign intelligence operation involving a suspected Chinese spy.

Huang, who co-founded Nvidia in 1993, has stepped down as president, CEO, and a member of the board of directors.

Nvidia confirmed that Colette Kress, its executive vice president and chief financial officer, will serve as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent replacement.

Kress joined Nvidia in September 2013 and has overseen its financial operations during a period of significant growth. She will retain her existing responsibilities while serving as interim CEO, with the company considering both internal and external candidates for the permanent position.

The leadership change follows reports that Huang had an extramarital relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based technology company Megaspeed, whom US intelligence officials reportedly suspected of working for Chinese intelligence.

The two are not related, and the allegations prompted Nvidia’s board to announce an independent investigation on November 8, overseen by outside legal counsel.

At the time, Nvidia said it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets had been compromised. The company also expressed confidence in Huang’s leadership and confirmed that he would cooperate with the investigation.

Huang subsequently acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees, apologizing to his family and colleagues while maintaining his commitment to the company.

The controversy also attracted attention from US lawmakers, with three House committee chairs requesting a closed-door briefing with Huang on November 18 to discuss his interactions with Alice Huang and Nvidia’s security procedures.

China’s Foreign Ministry has rejected the espionage allegations, while Singaporean authorities have said they did not identify Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset.

Shortly after the investigation, it was reported that Huang had a second affair with an executive from the company, and on November 12, Reuters reported a third alleged affair with a second employee.

Nvidia’s November 12 announcement did not address the status of its investigation or whether Huang would continue cooperating following his departure.

Alongside Kress’s appointment, Nvidia announced that Harvey C. Jones would become the company’s first-ever chair of the board.

Jones, who has more than 30 years of experience in the technology industry, said the company expects its operations and product roadmap to continue without interruption.

“Colette has helped lead NVIDIA through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position,” Jones said.

“She is supported by an experienced leadership team that has been instrumental in executing NVIDIA’s strategy and advancing its technology roadmap. The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company’s operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption.”

Kress also addressed her appointment, thanking Huang for his contributions to Nvidia over the past three decades.

“NVIDIA’s strength has always come from its people, and together with our leadership team, I am committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years,” she said.

“We will continue advancing the work that has made NVIDIA a leader in accelerated computing and AI, and execute on the opportunities ahead. Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders.”

Huang’s departure comes as Nvidia faces increased scrutiny over its leadership and business dealings with China, with its advanced AI chips remaining subject to US export restrictions.

#10741. ChatGPT as a therapist? New study reveals serious ethical risks

As more people seek mental health advice from ChatGPT and other large language models (LLMs), new research suggests these AI chatbots may not be ready for that role. The study found that even when instructed to use established psychotherapy approaches, the systems consistently fail to meet professional ethics standards set by organizations such as the American Psychological Association.

Researchers from Brown University, working closely with mental health professionals, identified repeated patterns of problematic behavior. In testing, chatbots mishandled crisis situations, gave responses that reinforced harmful beliefs about users or others, and used language that created the appearance of empathy without genuine understanding.

"In this work, we present a practitioner-informed framework of 15 ethical risks to demonstrate how LLM counselors violate ethical standards in mental health practice by mapping the model's behavior to specific ethical violations," the researchers wrote in their study. "We call on future work to create ethical, educational and legal standards for LLM counselors -- standards that are reflective of the quality and rigor of care required for human-facilitated psychotherapy."

The findings were presented at the AAAI/ACM Conference on Artificial Intelligence, Ethics and Society. The research team is affiliated with Brown's Center for Technological Responsibility, Reimagination and Redesign.

Zainab Iftikhar, a Ph.D. candidate in computer science at Brown who led the study, set out to examine whether carefully worded prompts could guide AI systems to behave more ethically in mental health settings. Prompts are written instructions designed to steer a model's output without retraining it or adding new data.

"Prompts are instructions that are given to the model to guide its behavior for achieving a specific task," Iftikhar said. "You don't change the underlying model or provide new data, but the prompt helps guide the model's output based on its pre-existing knowledge and learned patterns.

"For example, a user might prompt the model with: 'Act as a cognitive behavioral therapist to help me reframe my thoughts,' or 'Use principles of dialectical behavior therapy to assist me in understanding and managing my emotions.' While these models do not actually perform these therapeutic techniques like a human would, they rather use their learned patterns to generate responses that align with the concepts of CBT or DBT based on the input prompt provided."

People regularly share these prompt strategies on platforms like TikTok, Instagram, and Reddit. Beyond individual experimentation, many consumer facing mental health chatbots are built by applying therapy related prompts to general purpose LLMs. That makes it especially important to understand whether prompting alone can make AI counseling safer.

Testing AI Chatbots in Simulated Counseling

To evaluate the systems, the researchers observed seven trained peer counselors who had experience with cognitive behavioral therapy. These counselors conducted self counseling sessions with AI models prompted to act as CBT therapists. The models tested included versions of OpenAI's GPT Series, Anthropic's Claude, and Meta's Llama.

The team then selected simulated chats based on real human counseling conversations. Three licensed clinical psychologists reviewed those transcripts to flag possible ethical violations.

The analysis uncovered 15 distinct risks grouped into five broad categories:

The Accountability Gap in AI Mental Health

Iftikhar noted that human therapists can also make mistakes. The key difference is oversight.

"For human therapists, there are governing boards and mechanisms for providers to be held professionally liable for mistreatment and malpractice," Iftikhar said. "But when LLM counselors make these violations, there are no established regulatory frameworks."

The researchers emphasize that their findings do not suggest AI has no place in mental health care. Tools powered by artificial intelligence could help expand access, particularly for people who face high costs or limited availability of licensed professionals. However, the study highlights the need for clear safeguards, responsible deployment, and stronger regulatory structures before relying on these systems in high stakes situations.

For now, Iftikhar hopes the work encourages caution.

"If you're talking to a chatbot about mental health, these are some things that people should be looking out for," she said.

Ellie Pavlick, a Brown computer science professor who was not involved in the research, said the study underscores the importance of carefully examining AI systems used in sensitive areas like mental health. Pavlick leads ARIA, a National Science Foundation AI research institute at Brown focused on building trustworthy AI assistants.

"The reality of AI today is that it's far easier to build and deploy systems than to evaluate and understand them," Pavlick said. "This paper required a team of clinical experts and a study that lasted for more than a year in order to demonstrate these risks. Most work in AI today is evaluated using automatic metrics which, by design, are static and lack a human in the loop."

She added that the study could serve as a model for future research aimed at improving safety in AI mental health tools.

"There is a real opportunity for AI to play a role in combating the mental health crisis that our society is facing, but it's of the utmost importance that we take the time to really critique and evaluate our systems every step of the way to avoid doing more harm than good," Pavlick said. "This work offers a good example of what that can look like."

#10739. Scientists built the hardest AI test ever and the results are surprising

As artificial intelligence systems began scoring extremely high on long used academic benchmarks, researchers noticed a growing issue. The tests that once challenged machines were no longer difficult enough. Well known evaluations such as the Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) exam, which had previously been seen as demanding, now fail to properly measure the capabilities of today's advanced AI models.

To solve this problem, a worldwide group of nearly 1,000 researchers, including a professor from Texas A&M University, developed a new type of test. Their goal was to build an exam that is broad, difficult, and grounded in expert human knowledge in ways that current AI systems still struggle to handle.

The result is "Humanity's Last Exam" (HLE), a 2,500 question assessment covering mathematics, humanities, natural sciences, ancient languages, and a wide range of highly specialized academic fields. Details of the project appear in a paper published in Nature, and additional information about the exam is available at lastexam.ai.

Among the many contributors is Dr. Tung Nguyen, instructional associate professor in the Department of Computer Science and Engineering at Texas A&M. Nguyen helped write and refine many of the exam questions.

"When AI systems start performing extremely well on human benchmarks, it's tempting to think they're approaching human-level understanding," Nguyen said. "But HLE reminds us that intelligence isn't just about pattern recognition -- it's about depth, context and specialized expertise."

The purpose of the exam was not to trick or defeat human test takers. Instead, the goal was to carefully identify areas where AI systems still fall short.

Specialists from around the world wrote and reviewed the questions included in Humanity's Last Exam. Each problem was carefully designed so it has one clear, verifiable answer. The questions were also crafted to prevent quick solutions through simple internet searches.

The topics come from advanced academic challenges. Some tasks involve translating ancient Palmyrene inscriptions, while others require identifying tiny anatomical structures in birds or analyzing detailed features of Biblical Hebrew pronunciation.

Researchers tested every question against leading AI systems. If any model was able to answer a question correctly, that question was removed from the final exam. This process ensured the test remained just beyond what current AI systems can reliably solve.

Early testing confirmed that the strategy worked. Even powerful AI models struggled with the exam. GPT-4o achieved a score of 2.7 percent, while Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 4.1 percent. OpenAI's o1 model performed somewhat better with 8 percent. The most capable systems so far, including Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 4.6, have reached accuracy levels between about 40 percent and 50 percent.

Nguyen explained that the issue of AI surpassing older tests is more than a technical concern. He contributed 73 of the 2,500 publicly available questions in HLE, the second highest number among contributors, and wrote the most questions related to mathematics and computer science.

"Without accurate assessment tools, policymakers, developers and users risk misinterpreting what AI systems can actually do," he said. "Benchmarks provide the foundation for measuring progress and identifying risks."

According to the research team, high scores on tests originally designed for humans do not necessarily indicate genuine intelligence. Those benchmarks mainly measure how well AI can complete specific tasks created for human learners, rather than capturing deeper understanding.

Despite the dramatic name, Humanity's Last Exam is not meant to suggest that humans are becoming obsolete. Instead, it highlights the large amount of knowledge and expertise that still remains uniquely human.

"This isn't a race against AI," Nguyen said. "It's a method for understanding where these systems are strong and where they struggle. That understanding helps us build safer, more reliable technologies. And, importantly, it reminds us why human expertise still matters."

Humanity's Last Exam is designed to serve as a durable and transparent benchmark for future AI systems. To support that goal, the researchers have released some questions publicly while keeping the majority hidden so that AI models cannot simply memorize the answers.

"For now, Humanity's Last Exam stands as one of the clearest assessments of the gap between AI and human intelligence," Nguyen said, "and despite rapid technological advances, it remains wide."

Nguyen emphasized that the scale of the project demonstrates the value of collaboration across disciplines and countries.

"What made this project extraordinary was the scale," he said. "Experts from nearly every discipline contributed. It wasn't just computer scientists; it was historians, physicists, linguists, medical researchers. That diversity is exactly what exposes the gaps in today's AI systems -- perhaps ironically, it's humans working together."

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10737. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.
